Which means
\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{a}}{\bm{a}}^{\top}{\bm{a}}{\bm{a}}^{\top}{\bm{a}}{\bm{a}}^{\top}\right]=\left(\frac{1}{h}+O(\frac{1}{h^{2}})\right){\bm{I}}_{h}
, taking it into
T_{16}

\displaystyle T_{16}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{n^{4}h^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{h}+O(\frac{1}{h^{2}})\right)tr\mathbb{E}\left[{{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}}^{\top}{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}{{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}}\right]

(172)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{n^{4}h^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{h}+O(\frac{1}{h^{2}})\right)tr\mathbb{E}\left[{{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}}{{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}}^{\top}{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{n^{4}h^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{h}+O(\frac{1}{h^{2}})\right)tr\mathbb{E}\left[{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{n^{4}h^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{h}+O(\frac{1}{h^{2}})\right)tr\mathbb{E}\left[{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{n^{4}h^{2}d}\left(\frac{1}{h}+O(\frac{1}{h^{2}})\right)tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

For
tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]
, we have

\displaystyle tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{y}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle+tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle+tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle+tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle+tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle+tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle+tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle+tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

It is easy to see

\displaystyle tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{d}tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert{\bm{X}}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert{\bm{X}}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert{\bm{X}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=O(n^{3})

It is also easy to see
tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left\lVert{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leq O(n^{3})
.

Here we focus on computing

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right],tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

since we have

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]=tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

For
tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]
, we have

\displaystyle tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

(173)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[tr\left({\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right)tr\left({\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert{\bm{X}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i,j}X_{ij}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{p,q}\left(X^{\top}X\right)_{pq}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{e}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i,j}X_{ij}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{p=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{n}X_{mp}^{2}\right)^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q\\
p\neq q\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}X_{kp}X_{kq}\right)^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\rho_{e}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{11}^{2}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{n}X_{mp}^{2}\right)^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q\\
p\neq q\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}X_{kp}X_{kq}\right)^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle n\rho_{e}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{11}^{2}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{n}X_{mp}^{2}\right)^{2}\right)\right]

(174)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle nd\times\left[(d-1)(3n+n^{2}-n)+15+2\times(n-1)\times 3+n^{2}-2\times(n-1)-1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{3}d\rho_{e}^{2}+O(n^{2}d)

\displaystyle n\rho_{e}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{11}^{2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q\\
p\neq q\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}X_{kp}X_{kq}\right)^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=n\rho_{e}^{2}\times\left[(d^{2}-d-2)n+2\times 3n\right]

(175)

\displaystyle=n^{2}d^{2}\rho_{e}^{2}+O(n^{2}d)

By (
174
) and (
175
)
we have

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\xi}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]=n^{3}d\rho_{e}^{2}+n^{2}d^{2}\rho_{e}^{2}+O(n^{2}d)

(176)

For
tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]
, we have

\displaystyle tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]

(177)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\lVert{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{d}\sum_{s=1}^{d}\sum_{q=1}^{n}X_{ki}X_{km}\beta^{*}_{m}\beta^{*^{\top}}_{s}X_{qs}X_{qj}\right)^{2}.

Case 1

i\neq j,m=s
. It is easy to see in this case, the main term holds when
3\leq m\leq d
and
k\neq q
, since other conditions will only have up to
O(n^{2}d)
. Combining with the condition
3\leq m\leq d
, we have

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]_{1}=O(n^{2}d).

Case 2

i\neq j,m\neq s.
It is easy to see in this case, the main term holds when
s\geq 2,m\geq 2
, and other conditions will only have up to
O(n^{2}d)
. Combining with the condition
s\geq 2,m\geq 2
, we have

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]_{2}=4n^{2}d^{2}+O(n^{2}d).

Case 3

i=j,m=s.
In this case, the main term still holds when
3\leq m\leq d
and
k\neq q
. We have

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]_{3}=O(n^{2}d).

Case 4

i=j,m\neq s.
In this case, the main term holds when
s\geq 2,m\geq 2
, we have

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]_{4}=O(n^{2}d).

By Case 1 to Case 4, We finally get

tr\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]=4n^{2}d^{2}+O(n^{2}d)

(178)

Here, we take (
176
) and(
178
), we finally get

\displaystyle T_{16}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{n^{4}h^{2}d}\left(\frac{1}{h}+O(\frac{1}{h^{2}})\right)\left[(2n^{3}d+2n^{2}d^{2})\rho_{e}^{2}+4n^{2}d^{2}+O(n^{2}d)\right]

(179)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}\rho_{e}^{2}}{nh^{e}}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}d\rho_{e}^{2}}{n^{2}h^{3}}+\frac{4\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}d}{n^{2}h^{3}}+O\left(\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{h^{5}}\right)

\square

All terms involve the product of an odd number of identical random matrices with zero mean, and due to their independence from other random matrices, these terms are all
0
.
\square

Analysis of
T_{18}
,
T_{19}
,
T_{22}
and
T_{23}
.

We take
T_{18}
as an example.

\displaystyle T_{18}

\displaystyle=tr\left(\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{W}}_{1}^{0},{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0},{\bm{a}},{\bm{\xi}},{\bm{X}}}\left[\frac{\eta_{1}}{nh}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{a}}^{\top}{{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}}^{\top}{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}{\bm{a}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}\right]\right)

(180)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{nh}\mathbb{E}tr\left[{{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}}^{\top}{\bm{W}}_{2}^{0}{\bm{a}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{a}}^{\top}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{nh}\mathbb{E}tr\left[{\bm{a}}^{\top}{\bm{a}}{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{nh}\mathbb{E}tr\left[{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{nh}\mathbb{E}tr\left[{\bm{y}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{nh}\mathbb{E}tr\left[{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{nh}\mathbb{E}tr\left[{\bm{X}}^{\top}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}^{*}{\bm{\beta}}^{*^{\top}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{h}

Similar to
T_{18}
, we can get

\displaystyle T_{19}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{2}}{d}

(181)

\displaystyle T_{22}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}}{h}

(182)

\displaystyle T_{23}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{2}}{d}

(183)

\square

Finally, we get the exact loss

\displaystyle L_{\text{three-layer}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{h^{2}}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}d(1+\rho_{e}^{2})}{h^{2}n}-2\frac{\eta_{1}}{h}

(184)

\displaystyle+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{hd}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}(1+\rho_{e}^{2})}{nh}+\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}(1+\rho_{e}^{2})}{nd}-2\frac{\eta_{2}}{d}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}{hd}+\frac{2\eta_{1}\eta_{2}(1+\rho_{e}^{2})}{nh}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}\rho_{e}^{2}}{nh^{3}}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}d\rho_{e}^{2}}{n^{2}h^{3}}+\frac{4\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}d}{n^{2}h^{3}}

\displaystyle+1+O(\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{h^{3}})+O(\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{h^{3}})+O(\frac{\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}{h^{3}})+O\left(\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{h^{5}}\right)

Under Assumption
E.1
, we have

\displaystyle L_{\text{three-layer}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{h^{2}}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}(1+\rho_{e}^{2})}{hn}-2\frac{\eta_{1}}{h}

(185)

\displaystyle+\frac{2\eta_{2}^{2}}{h^{2}}+\frac{2\eta_{2}^{2}(1+\rho_{e}^{2})}{nh}-2\frac{\eta_{2}}{h}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}{h^{2}}+\frac{2\eta_{1}\eta_{2}(1+\rho_{e}^{2})}{nh}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}\rho_{e}^{2}}{nh^{3}}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}\rho_{e}^{2}}{n^{2}h^{2}}+\frac{4\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{n^{2}h^{2}}

\displaystyle+1+O(\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}}{h^{3}})+O(\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{h^{3}})+O(\frac{\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}{h^{3}})+O\left(\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}^{2}}{h^{5}}\right)

\square

Appendix F
Additional Experiments

F.1
Spectral Analysis

To better understand Proposition
5.1
, in this subsection, we perform spectral analysis of the key matrices like
\{{\bm{A}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2},\{{\bm{B}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2},\{{\bm{G}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
,
\{\widetilde{{\bm{A}}_{l}^{1}}\}_{l=1}^{2}
,
\{\widetilde{{\bm{B}}_{l}^{1}}\}_{l=1}^{2}
,
\{\widetilde{{\bm{G}}_{l}^{1}}\}_{l=1}^{2}
arising after one-step and two-step updates in a two-layer linear neural network under orthogonal initialization. In Figure
3
, we consider
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=h^{\frac{3}{2}}
with
h=1000
, we visualize the empirical spectral densities (ESDs) of the weight matrices, gradient matrices, and the decomposed gradient components represented as
{\bm{A}}
and
{\bm{B}}
. Take
\{{\bm{A}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2},\{{\bm{B}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2},\{{\bm{G}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
as examples, we find that the eigenvalue scales of
\{{\bm{A}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
and
\{{\bm{G}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
are comparable, and are larger than those of
\{{\bm{B}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
by an
O(h)
factor. This matches our norm analysis in Section
C.1.2
and
C.2.1
, showing that
\left\lVert{\bm{A}}_{l}^{0}\right\rVert
exceeds
\left\lVert{\bm{B}}_{l}^{0}\right\rVert
) by
O(\sqrt{h})
, since the ESD is computed from the eigenvalues of
{\bm{W}}^{\top}{\bm{W}}
. We visualize the norm gap in Figure
3(q)
and
3(r)
, which also confirm that the eigenvalue scales of
\{{\bm{A}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
and
\{{\bm{G}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
are comparable, and are larger than those of
\{{\bm{B}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
in magnitude, we also find the eigenvalue scales of
\{\eta_{l}{\bm{G}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
and
\{\eta_{l}{\bm{A}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
are comparable to
\{\widetilde{{\bm{W}}_{l}^{1}}\}_{l=1}^{2}
, which matches our Proposition
5.1
. Consequently, the ESDs provide an intuitive explanation for why
\{{\bm{A}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
and
\{{\bm{G}}_{l}^{0}\}_{l=1}^{2}
are close in norm, supporting our use of the approximate gradient when deriving the one-step and two-step exact losses. A similar phenomenon holds for
\{\widetilde{{\bm{A}}_{l}^{1}}\}_{l=1}^{2}
and
\{\overline{{\bm{G}}_{l}^{1}}\}_{l=1}^{2}
, relative to
\{\widetilde{{\bm{B}}_{l}^{1}}\}_{l=1}^{2}
.

F.2
Theoretical Simulation

Here we present more experimental results.

Orthogonal initialization.

In Figure
5
and
7
, Under orthogonal initialization, we set
h=1000
and conducted experiments for
steps

\in\{1,2,4,8\}
under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}\leq h^{\frac{3}{2}}
. Consistent with our earlier findings: after two updates the model exhibits local optimality at balanced layer-wise learning rates. We also try different
h
and find that when
h
satisfy the condition on
h
in Corollary like Corollary
5.4
, the balanced learning-rate allocation is locally optimal, otherwise not. See Figure
11
,
12
and
13
.

Orthogonal initialization.

We also ran the same set of experiments under Gaussian initialization In Figure
6
and
8
, with
h=1000
and
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}\leq h^{\frac{2}{3}}
, again for
steps

\in\{1,2,4,8\}
. The results mirror those under orthogonal initialization: balanced learning rates become locally optimal after two updates, whereas after a single update an asymmetric learning-rate allocation performs better, which is consistent with the special cases of Theorem
E.4
and Theorem
E.6
for two-layer and three-layer neural networks.

More discussions in Section
7
.

For the question about our paper shows symmetric learning rates are suboptimal for one step but optimal for two steps, which may be not exactly the same as using asymmetric learning rates early and symmetric ones later. Here we agree that our theory focuses on the result that symmetric learning rates are suboptimal for a single update step but become optimal after two steps. This suggests that 1. asymmetric learning rates may be preferable at the very beginning of training, 2. symmetric learning rates become optimal as cross-layer interactions develop over subsequent steps, even if the initial learning-rate allocation is not optimal. We believe this also points to a practical strategy that use asymmetric learning rates early in training and more symmetric ones later.

To better connect these two regimes, in Figure
16
(a)(b) we consider a three layer linear network in which the first step uses an asymmetric learning rate allocation by training only the first layer. For the second step, we then search over the test loss as a function of
\eta_{1}
under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C
. We find that the same transition still appears: from asymmetry at the first step to balance at the second step.

In Figure
16
(c)(d)(e), we consider a 3-layer CNN whose first two layers are convolutional layers and whose final layer is a fixed linear readout layer. We consider a synthetic binary image classification problem on
16\times 16
grayscale images. Each sample belongs to one of two classes: Class
-1
: an image containing a horizontal bar. Class
+1
: an image containing a vertical bar. We consider two trainable
3\times 3
convolutional layers with no bias: the first maps from 1 input channel to 8 hidden channels, and the second maps from 8 channels to 8 channels, with a ReLU activation after each convolution. The resulting feature map is then globally average pooled over the spatial dimensions, producing an 8-dimensional representation, which is fed into a fixed random linear readout to produce a single scalar output.

In Figure
16
(c)(d), for the first step, we consider only updating the first(second) layer, using learning rate
C
. For the second step, we study layer-wise learning-rate allocation under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.
Although the optimum no longer occurs exactly at
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}
because of the changed architecture and the presence of nonlinearities, the optimal performance is still attained when
\eta_{1}\approx\eta_{2}
. This indicates that, after an asymmetric first step, learning rates that are approximately symmetric still remain preferable. In Figure
16
(e), We study a 3-layer CNN in which the second update step uses a symmetric learning-rate allocation,
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=\frac{C}{2},
while the first step is optimized under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.
We find training the second layer (corresponding to
\eta_{1}=0
) at the first step is better than the symmetric allocation. Thus, the CNN experiments lead to similar conclusions as in the 3-layer linear neural network setting, further reinforcing our claim.

\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}

Figure 14
:

4-layer and 8-layer NN under orthogonal initialization for 1 and 2-step updates.
For 4-NN, we set
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\eta_{3}=C\leq O(h^{\frac{2}{3}})
with
h=1000
and we set
\eta_{2}=\eta_{3}=\frac{C-\eta_{1}}{2}.
For 8-NN, we set
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\eta_{3}+\eta_{4}+\eta_{5}+\eta_{6}+\eta_{7}=C\leq O(h^{\frac{2}{3}})
with
h=1000
and we set
\eta_{2}=\eta_{3}=\eta_{4}=\eta_{5}=\eta_{6}=\eta_{7}=\frac{C-\eta_{1}}{6}.

:

3-NN nonlinear under orthogonal initialization for 1 and 8-step updates.

f({\bm{x}}_{i})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\sigma(\sigma({\bm{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{W}}_{1}){\bm{W}}_{2}){\bm{a}},
and the teacher model is

Figure 16
:

(a)
3-layer NN under orthogonal initialization for special 2-step update. Here we set
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C\leq O(h^{\frac{2}{3}})
with
h=1000.
We set the first step update step to use an asymmetric learning-rate allocation: the first layer is updated with learning rate C, while the second layer is not trained. For the second update step, we then optimize under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.

(b)
3-layer NN under orthogonal initialization for special 2-step update. Here we set
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C
. We set the second update step to use an symmetric learning-rate allocation:
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=\frac{C}{2}
. For the first update step, we optimize under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.

(c)
3-layer CNN for special 2-step update. Here we set
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.
We set the first update step to use an asymmetric learning-rate allocation: the first layer is updated with learning rate C, while the second layer is not trained. For the second update step, we then optimize under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.

(d)
3-layer CNN for special 2-step update. Here we set
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.
We set the first update step to use an asymmetric learning-rate allocation: the second layer is updated with learning rate C, while the first layer is not trained. For the second update step, we then optimize under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C
.
(e)
3-layer CNN for special 2-step update. Here we set
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.
We set the second update step to use an symmetric learning-rate allocation:
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=\frac{C}{2}
. For the first update step, we optimize under the constraint
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=C.

Figure 17
:

Insights for designing layer-wise lr scheduler.
Here we condier
{\bm{W}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{60\times 100},{\bm{W}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{100\times 60}
and
{\bm{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{60\times 60}
, with
\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{1}\right\rVert_{F}=1
and
\left\lVert{\bm{W}}_{2}\right\rVert_{F}=6
at initialization. We use 100 training samples and 20 test samples, base
lr=0.0001
, adopt the MSE loss, and train the model using gradient descent for 200 iterations.
